Purpose of Report 
1. This report presents to Council for approval, the General Fund revenue 
budget for the next financial year (2026/27) along with the 5-year Medium 
Term Financial Plan (MTFP) covering the period 2026/27 to 2030/31. 
2. The report also presents the proposed Council Tax levels for the 2026/27 
financial year including the Greater London Authority (GLA) precept. 
3. The Council has a statutory obligation to undertake an annual budget 
process and to set a balanced budget for the forthcoming financial year. 
4. The report also ask for approval of the Schools’ (Dedicated Schools Grant) 
Budget. 
5. The Budget has been developed alongside the Ten-Year Capital Strategy 
and Capital programme 2026/27 to 2035/36 and the Ten-Year Treasury 
Management Strategy 2026/27 to 2035/36; these are both separate reports 
on this agenda. 


=== PDF PAGE 2 ===
 
Recommendations 
6. Council is recommended to: 
i. Set the Council Tax Requirement for Enfield at £184.874m for 
2026/27 as set out in the Statutory Budget Determination in Appendix 
1 to the report. 
ii. Set the Council Tax at Band D for Enfield Council for 2026/27 at 
£1,757.16. 
iii. Note that the Band D property rate in 2026/27 for Enfield will be 
£1,757.16.  This includes a 2.99% general Council Tax increase and a 
2.00% Adult Social Care Precept and represents an increase of £1.60 
per week. 
iv. Note the Council will levy a Council Tax of £510.51 at Band D on 
behalf of the Greater London Authority which is a 4.10% increase: 
equivalent to an increase of 39p per week for a Band D property. 
v. Note that the combined increases will lead to a Band D property total 
of £2,267.67, a 4.79% increase on the 2025/26 level, equivalent to an 
increase of £1.99 per week for a Band D property. 
vi. Note the Council’s Section 151 Officer’s statutory Section 25 
Statement at Appendix 2 which provides their opinion on the 
robustness of the budget and the adequacy of the Council’s reserves 
when making final decisions on the 2026/27 budget.  
vii. To agree to the proposed General Fund Net Revenue Budget of 
£438.969m for 2026/27. 
viii. Agree in principle the final list of efficiencies and savings proposals for 
2026/27 totalling £11.960m, as set out in Appendix 9, noting also that 
final decisions on these savings or income proposals will be subject to 
equality impact assessment, consultation and formal decision making 
in the usual way. 
ix. Approve the latest General Fund Medium-Term Financial Plan 
estimates for 2026/27 to 2030/31 set out in Table 3 and to note the 
estimated budget gap of £34.5m at the end of that period. 
x. Note the forecast levels of Council usable reserves as set out in 
Appendix 11b. 
xi. Approve the planned transfers to and from reserves in setting the 
budget for 2026/27 as set out in out paragraph 66. 
xii. Agree the Reserves policy for 2026/27 as set out in Appendix 11a 
xiii. Agree the proposed Fees and Charges set out in Appendix 12 and the 
continuation of the flexibility introduced in 2021/22 for these to be 
varied in year where appropriate. 
xiv. Approve the Flexible Use of Capital Receipts Policy as at Appendix 13 
and to approve that any in-year changes required to the Flexible Use 
of Capital Receipts Policy is delegated to the Chief Finance Officer in 
consultation with the Cabinet Member for Finance and Procurement 
and reported to Cabinet in the quarterly Revenue Monitoring reports. 

=== PDF PAGE 3 ===
xv. To note the feedback from the Budget Consultation at Appendix 14a.  
xvi. To note the minutes of the Overview and Scrutiny Committee Meeting 
on 19 January 2026 following its consideration of the draft budget 
which are set out in Appendix 14b of this report. 
xvii. Agree the schools (DSG) Budget for 2026/27 as set out in paragraphs 
94 to 112 and Appendix 15. 
 
Executive Summary, Background and Options 
7. The government has fundamentally changed the way funding is allocated to 
councils through the Fair Funding 2.0 review, reflecting longstanding 
lobbying by elected members and officers for funding that better aligns with 
levels of deprivation and need.  Following a consultation during the 
summer, the detailed funding outcomes have now been published in the 
Final Local Government Finance Settlement. 
8. The significant increase in core funding and the council's disciplined 
financial approach, has been able to protect frontline services, with no 
reductions to the essential support our residents rely on being proposed in 
this budget. 
9. The Council’s funding is forecast to increase by £65m between 2025/26 and 
2026/27 - £54.6m increase in government funding and £10.3m increase in 
Council Tax income.  
10. After core funding increases for 2026/27, the net revenue budget is 
proposed to increase by £90.2m from £348.7m in 2025/26 to £438.9m in 
2026/27 (£65m increase in funding and council tax income, and £21m due 
to realigning our methodology of calculating net revenue budget in order to 
bring it closer to the way the government calculates Core Spending power).   
11. This growth is funding significant and increasing demand for social care and 
homelessness support which is being experienced across the sector.  
12. The most significant growth areas and pressures for 2026/27 are forecast to 
be: 
• £19.3m Adult Social Care from demographic demand, inflation, and 
Bad Debt Provision 
• £6.0m Children’s Social Care from inflation and demand for external 
care purchasing 
• £7.4m for pay award 
• £5.4m for increased demand in Temporary Accommodation 
• £1.6m for Capital Financing 
• £2.5m for NLWA and Concessionary Fare levies 
• £5.0m to recognise risk of further demand service increases 
• £2.5m for contract inflation 

=== PDF PAGE 4 ===
13. The Children’s, Families & Youth Grant funds new long term investment into 
Children’s Social Care to strengthen support for children, young people and 
families, with a focus on prevention, reducing inequalities, and improving life 
chances and help people facing financial crisis.  
14. The £7.4m per annum Crisis & Resilience Fund provides Enfield with 
funding to deliver preventive support and help residents facing financial 
shocks, replacing the Household Support Fund and Discretionary Housing 
Payments.  
15. The first multi-year Settlement in a decade gives local authorities like 
Enfield the certainty to plan for investment in longer term early intervention 
measures that tackle the causes of deprivation. The Medium-Term 
Financial Plan reflects that the Council will have a balanced budget for the 
next three years but a gap £34.5m remaining in years four and five.  The 
settlement, however, does not immediately undo years of underfunding, the 
council must keep financial discipline to protect front line investment. 
16. The Council is now in a far stronger position to manage future pressures but 
recognises that the next three years must be utilised to transform and 
prepare for future challenges. This settlement is a once in a generation 
opportunity to do that. 
17. As set out in the Council’s Financial Resilience Framework (Appendix 3), 
attaining “resilient” status is within reach but only if the Council continues 
with its current financial discipline.  The Council’s current financial resilience 
remains classed as “vulnerable”, with the aim of moving to “stable” during 
2026/27.   
18. It is imperative that the Council delivers the current and proposed savings 
programme and future efficiency targets have been built in to achieve a 
balanced budget over the 3-year settlement period. 
19. The Council is required to undertake a budget consultation which 
commenced in December and closed in early January. The feedback 
received has been incorporated into this final budget. 
20. The Council’s Section 151 Officer (CFO) is required by law to give their 
professional opinion on the robustness of the budget proposals and the 
adequacy of reserves given the financial risks that the Council is facing. 
Members should have due regard for this Section 25 statement, attached at 
Appendix 2, when making their decision. 
21. The proposed General Fund Revenue Budget for 2026/27 has been 
developed alongside the ten-year Capital Strategy, together with the 
proposed capital programme 2026/27 to 2035/36 to support the strategy.  
The proposed budget incorporates the revenue impacts of the proposed 
capital budget for 2026/27. 
22. The report also presents the Schools Budget for 2026/27 for 
recommendation for approval to Council.  The Department for Education 
(DfE) provided the Dedicated Schools Grant (DSG) settlement on the 17 
December.  The allocations are used in the schools' budget allocation 
model which is based on the National Funding Formula to allocate 
individual school budgets.  The schools’ budgets are reported to the 
Schools Forum in January prior to submission to the DfE and the final 
allocations are confirmed in February. 

=== PDF PAGE 5 ===
23. Whilst the DSG deficit does not directly impact on the Council’s revenue 
reserves, the deficit still requires cash, which is in effect taken from other 
reserves. i.e. the DSG deficit either reduces cash surpluses available to 
invest or increases external borrowing. Conservatively, a yield or interest 
cost of 3.85% and a circa £23m deficit will cost the council £0.9m in interest 
over a full year. 
24. The government announced in the final settlement a new High Needs 
Stability grant that will fund 90% of DSG deficits subject to conditions. This 
new grant will significantly reduce the financial risk that the Council was 
facing when the DSG deficit statutory override was due to cease in March 
2028. 
 
Setting the 2026/27 budget 
25. The proposals within this report are the culmination of the 2026/27 Revenue 
and Capital budget planning process and provide information on: 
• the financial outlook for the local government sector and the Council 
specifically; 
• the details of the Local Government Finance Settlement and changes 
to the Council’s funding; 
• changes to the budget since the December draft budget report; 
• the outcome of the recent budget consultation exercise; 
• the proposed Council Tax levels; 
• the Medium-Term Financial Plan providing financial best estimates for 
the next five years; 
• the Council’s level of reserves to underpin its continued financial 
sustainability. 
26. The robustness of the savings and estimates have been tested as part of 
the budget cycle including via Executive Management Board, Scrutiny and 
Cabinet and Cabinet Member briefings.   
27. The key objective of the Council’s Medium Term Financial Plan is financial 
resilience and protecting the services which are providing support to the 
most vulnerable in the Borough. 
 
Review of Overall Funding, Spending Assumptions and Savings Proposals 
Funding Assumptions 
28. The final budget as proposed is based on the announcement of the final 
Local Government Finance Settlement in February 2026 and finalised 
Council Tax and Business Rates estimates for 2026/27. 
29. The proposed General Fund revenue budget for 2026/27 assumes that the 
Council increases Council Tax by the threshold level before a referendum 
must be held.  For 2026/27, the threshold level allowable is 4.99% (2.99% 
core council tax and 2.00% for the Adult Social Care precept). 

=== PDF PAGE 6 ===
30. In line with statutory requirements, Members are required to approve the 
proposed Band D Council Tax for 2026/27 of £1,757.16, and to note the 
Mayor’s GLA precept of £510.51 which will be added to this amount. 
31. The government settlement for 2026/27 is the first multi-year settlement in a 
decade.  Along with the funding allocations for 2026/27, indicative funding 
levels have also been provided for 2027/28 & 2028/29. 
32. The outcome of the Fair Funding Review has been very positive for Enfield, 
where the government estimated that the borough has had the second 
highest increase in core spending power in the country and the highest in 
London.  This fundamental shift in resources finally recognises the needs of 
the borough and provides a certainty in the level of funding over the next 
three years, though it is still expected that the government will confirm the 
allocations for 2027/28 and 2028/29 on an annual basis.  This does provide 
some uncertainty, and the council has asked the government to strengthen 
its commitment to the published allocations for these future years. 
33. In addition, the government has sought to reduce the volume of individual 
grants that authorities receive.  More detail on the changes can be found in 
Appendix 7. 
34. The government’s assessment of the Council’s core spending power 
includes an estimate of our business rates income and similarly an estimate 
of our council tax income (with an assumption that the maximum 4.99% 
increase is applied).  The Council’s actual council tax income included for 
2026/27 is calculated using local data, whilst the business rates income is 
derived from the statutory NNDR1 return that was submitted to government 
by the 31 January 2026 deadline. 
35. The estimated funding levels for 2026/27 Council Tax were presented to 
Council on 21 January 2026 within the tax base report. 
36. The final LGFS announced a core spending power total of £442.8m which 
includes the Council’s Fair Funding Allocation (FFA), Local Authority Better 
Care Grant, Council Tax requirement, Homelessness, Rough Sleeping and 
Domestic Abuse grant, Families First Partnership Grant and the Recovery 
Grant. 
37. Our FFA can be further broken down into the Baseline Funding Level (BFL) 
which represents the minimum amount of funding a local authority is 
guaranteed to receive from the national business rates system, before 
taking into account how much business rates it actually collects locally.  
This will be made up of the Council’s Business Rates Baseline and top up 
grant.  The government’s assumptions for BFL are £50.2m for Business 
Rates and £91.1m top up for a total BFL of £141.3m. Revenue Support 
Grant of £75.3m is then added to arrive at the FFA figure of £216.6m. 
38. It is important to note that the government makes its own assumptions on 
the Baseline Funding Level and Council Tax requirement which are used as 
a guide but are replaced with local knowledge on what is actually happening 
within the borough. 
39. The Council’s Net Revenue Budget (NRB) has been set to align with the 
core spending power assessment with revised estimates for BFL and 
Council Tax. 

=== PDF PAGE 7 ===
40. In addition to core spending power, the government also announced other 
grant funding allocations for 2026/27, namely the consolidated Public 
Health grant, the Crisis Resilience Fund and Children’s, Families and Youth 
Grant.  Plus, the Council has received notification for two other grants, 
those being the Housing Benefit Subsidy Admin grant and the Extended 
Producer Responsibility grant.  Further details of all of the grants can be 
found in Appendix 7. 
41. Overall, the Council has seen an increase of £54.5m in FFA and grants 
funding.  Note that £21.3m of the change in the Net Revenue Budget is due 
to the reclassification of service and core grants.

42. The budget is balanced over the next three years, actual business rates 
performance can fluctuate, and the government’s safety net protection 
reduces from 100% in 2026/27 to 97% and then 92.5% in 2027/28 and 
2028/29 respectively.  Therefore, actual performance will continue to be 
monitored regularly, and the plan adjusted accordingly in future years. 
43. Further detail on each funding source and the changes to funding 
assumptions in the MTFP are summarised in Appendix 7. 
 
Investment, Inflation, Pay Award, Demography Spending Assumptions 
44. Growth and investment spending assumptions for 2026/27 were estimated 
in the February 2025 Council Report to be £23.6m.  These have since been 
reviewed and updated and now total £79.0m following the final Local 
Government Finance settlement (i.e. from increases in government funding, 
council tax and also efficiencies that are reinvested into services).   
45. £24.8m of investment expenditure is proposed in the final budget. This 
includes  

=== PDF PAGE 8 ===
• £4.4m is being set aside for early intervention and prevention of 
deprivation with more detailed plans to be developed over the coming 
months,  
• £5.5m for the Crisis & Resilience Fund (excluding the Discretionary 
Housing Payments element).  
• £6.7m for children’s social care transformation 
• and confirms funding for Summer University, investment in Waste 
Services and Trading Standards, as well as improvements to cyber 
security and various IT systems for costs relating to the Council’s ERP 
system upgrade. 
46. £16.3m of the above relating to new expenditure related to ringfenced grant 
conditions for the First Families First Partnership grant, Public Health grant, 
Homelessness & Rough Sleeping grant, Extended Producer 
Responsibilities and the Crisis Resilience Fund (see Investment to Address 
Deprivation and Support Vulnerable Residents section below).  In addition, 
to increase the amount of capital receipts available to reduce the Council’s 
level of debt, expenditure previously planned to be to be financed through 
the flexible use of capital receipts is being replaced with permanent base 
budget funding.  This includes £2.0m for the Digital Portfolio and £1.0m for 
transformation (which includes existing invest to save schemes). 
47. Demand pressures are continuing to increase. £54.2m of growth is forecast 
for 2026/27 when compared to the 2025/26 budget. 
48. The most significant growth areas and pressures for 2026/27 are forecast to 
be: 
• £19.3m Adult Social Care from demographic demand, inflation, and 
Bad Debt Provision. 
• £6.0m Children’s Social Care from inflation and demand for external 
care purchasing. 
• £7.4m for pay award. 
• £5.4m for increased demand in Temporary Accommodation. 
• £1.6m for Capital Financing. 
• £2.5m for NLWA and Concessionary Fare levies 
• £5.0m addition to the central contingency has been added since the 
draft budget. 
• £2.5m for contract inflation 
49. There are several reasons driving the gap, notably pay award, inflation, 
demography, homelessness, children's and adult social care services cost 
pressures.  The latest spending assumptions in the MTFP are summarised 
in Appendix 8. 
  

=== PDF PAGE 9 ===
Investment to Address Deprivation and Support Vulnerable Residents 
50. The Crisis and Resilience Fund (CRF) will be established to provide 
targeted support to residents and communities in the borough who are 
experiencing acute hardship or are at risk of crisis. This will build on the 
Council’s experience of administering previous schemes such as the 
Household Support Fund, but with a renewed focus on both immediate 
crisis intervention and longer-term resilience. The CRF will be designed to 
ensure that those most in need receive timely and effective assistance, 
while also strengthening the borough’s capacity to respond to future shocks. 
A key principle that will underpin the CRF will be the integration of crisis 
support with preventative measures. By embedding resilience-building 
activities within the Fund, the Council aims to reduce the recurrence of 
crisis and promote sustainable outcomes for vulnerable households.

52. Appendix 9 provides the full list of the proposals being put forward in this 
report for approval.  Note that the savings proposals set out in this report 
are being recommended for approval subject to consultation and 
assessment of the equality implications where relevant.  To maximise the 
financial benefit of these proposals it is recommended that, where 
applicable, they are implemented as soon as feasibly possible, which could 
mean that some could help mitigate the 2025/26 forecast overspend and 
the consequent draw on reserves. 
53. Since the December Cabinet report, the following amendments and 
additions have been made: 
• Two new invest to save proposals are put forward for approval, these 
being the introduction of padel courts in the borough and the 
insourcing of repairs to parks equipment. 
• The removal of two proposals put forward in the draft December 
budget following further due diligence totalling £0.2m. 
• Revised the saving adjustment to provide greater resilience of 
optimism bias and slippage in 2026/27. 

=== PDF PAGE 10 ===
• Recategorisation of the net benefit of Extended Producer 
Responsibility grant into the funding section. 
54. Some of these savings’ proposals will be subject to public consultation and 
responses, which will be considered and factored into the final budget 
proposals.  Further due diligence is also being undertaken to ensure that 
these proposals are robust and deliverable. 
55. Whilst robust due diligence has been undertaken, the risk exists that the 
identified savings may not be delivered due to circumstances beyond the 
Council’s control and for the first time a savings adjustment has been built 
into the budget to mitigate optimism bias and slippage into future years.  
There are also initiatives that have been put in place to mitigate budget 
pressures, and these are also dependent on external factors.  For example, 
the availability of properties for temporary accommodation (either 
acquisition or rented) and the levels of private market rents and the costs of 
building new homes.

56. The Council is committed to its responsibilities under the Public Sector Duty 
of the Equality Act 2010. These duties are set out in the Equalities Impact 
Implications section later in this report (paragraphs 136 to 141 below). 
57. For 2026/27 there are 35 individual savings and income proposals, and 
these have all been evaluated to determine whether an Equality Impact 
Assessment (EQIA) is required and there is paperwork in place to support 
this evaluation. 
58. Of the 35 proposals 7 have required a full EQIA.

Contingencies, Balances and Reserves 
61. Cabinet has previously agreed a high-level methodology to set minimum 
and target risk reserves.  The Council has made good progress towards 
reaching the minimum threshold balance for risk reserves.  For 2026/27 the 
minimum threshold balance for risk reserves is calculated as £42.9m 
(because the General Fund Balance is higher, so risk reserves can be 
lower) and the target balance is £90.6m.  The target risk reserve balance 
has not been revised for the impact of the new High Needs Stability grant at 
this time because as per government guidance the eligible deficit will only 
be determined following an assessment by the Department of Education 
and MHCLG.  The Council reserves strategy can be found in Appendix 11a. 
62. With the final settlement being better than the ‘favourable’ scenario, the 
budget over the life of the MTFP allows the council to strengthen its 
reserves position and maintain the General Fund balance at a minimum of 
5% of the net revenue budget.  Annual contributions to reserves have been 
built in which should enable the Council to reach Target Risk Reserves in 
three years, if there are no overspends in any of the years from 2026/27 
onwards.  The 5-year plan allows for an ongoing contribution of £5.0m per 
year beyond 2030/31. 
63. The Council’s policy will continue to be one of containing spending within 
the budgets set for each department, without recourse to the central 
contingency other than in exceptional circumstances.  The budget includes 
a central contingency for unforeseen circumstances and, in view of the level 
of risks and benchmarking against other London Boroughs, it is 
recommended that the central contingency be increased to £8.0m for 
2026/27 i.e. circa 1.7% of the net revenue budget.  The Council also holds 
centrally a few contingent items relating to spending requirements that are 
expected to arise at some point in the budget year but about which there is 
some uncertainty regarding the timing or magnitude of the financial impact. 
These will be allocated to services during the year. 
64. The future years gaps for 2029/30 and 2030/31 currently projected in the 
MTFP are expected to be met through savings, income and funding 
changes and will therefore not require a draw upon reserves. 
65. Earmarked reserves are held to meet the cost of specific one-off projects or 
specific risks.  A list of the Council’s Earmarked Reserves and the purposes 
for which they are held is set out in Appendix 11a. Planned movements in 
the reserve balances over the period of the MTFP are shown in Appendix 
11b. These are split between revenue and capital projects which are 
included in the MTFP and Capital Programme respectively. 
66. The planned transfers to and from reserves in setting the budget for 
2026/27 are: 
• £4.1m to increase the General Fund balance to £21.8m for 2026/27, in 
line with the policy of maintaining the balance at 5% of the net revenue 
budget. 
• £4.3m contribution to the Risk Reserve in line with the Council’s policy 
of rebuilding its risk reserves and annual progress towards achieving 
the target risk reserve level. 

=== PDF PAGE 14 ===
• £ 0.8m contribution to the Energetik shareholder reserve as set out in 
the Treasury Management Strategy. 
• £1.1m contribution to the Collection Fund equalisation reserve to 
mitigate against future fluctuations in collection fund performance and 
reductions in the government safety net for business rates funding.  
This £1.1m is the forecast 2025/26 surplus on the Collection Fund.

numerous factors could easily cause an overspend in 2026/27 or create a  
budget gap in the future, which the council will need to manage.

69. There are also key inherent risks that are not unique to Enfield, and which 
most London boroughs will face in setting their budgets, although the level 
of risks for each can be very different: 
i. Macro-economic factors - assumptions are built into the forecast for 
inflation and interest rates but these could change. 
ii. Energy costs inflation. 
iii. Pay award (currently assumed at 3.2%). 
iv. Optimism bias on savings identified and capacity to deliver the volume 
of savings, though an adjustment has been made to allow for the 
possibility of deferrals into future years. 
v. Demand increases beyond forecasted levels. 
70. The Government plans to establish two new negotiating bodies - the School 
Support Staff Negotiating Body (SSSNB) for non-teaching school staff and 
the Adult Social Care Negotiating Body (ASCNB) for adult social care 
workers - through the Employment Rights Bill introduced on 10 October 
2024. These bodies, which fall outside current national agreements (NJC 
and JNC), are set to begin in the 2027/28 academic year (SSSNB) and in 
2028 (ASCNB). It remains unclear whether pay and other terms from these 
bodies will be fully funded or how any unfunded costs may affect councils 
and providers. 
71. The budget process for 2026/27 has sought to identify the financial risks 
facing the Council currently and where possible to also quantify them.  
Identification is one of the key steps in managing risk and this will be 
supplemented by regular review.

Flexible Use of Capital Receipts 
74. With effect from 2016/17 the Government provided a general capitalisation 
directive to all councils, enabling them to utilise new capital receipts to 
finance projects that are designed to generate ongoing revenue savings in 
the delivery of public services and/or transform service delivery in a way 
that reduces costs or demand for services in future years for any of the 
public sector delivery partners. 
75. The Local government finance policy statement 2025/26 (published on the 
28 November 2024) stated that the government will extend the flexible use 
of capital receipts to 2030.  The government will also remove the restriction 
with respect to redundancy costs, imposed from April 2022, that limits the 
use of the flexibility to statutory redundancy costs only. 
76. Local authorities must produce a strategy which discloses the individual 
projects that will be funded, or part funded, through capital receipts flexibility 
and this must be approved by full Council or the equivalent.  As a minimum, 
the strategy should list each project that plans to make use of the capital 
receipts flexibility and provide details, on a project-by-project basis, of the 
expected savings or service transformation that will result.  The strategy 
should report the impact on the local authority’s Prudential Indicators for the 
forthcoming year and subsequent years. 
77. The Council remains mindful of over reliance on, and sustainability of this 
one-off funding, and is focused on longer term solutions to fund any 
ongoing pressures and make the budget more robust.  To this end, £3m 
permanent revenue budgets have been built into the budget for Digital 
portfolio and transformation, replacing the capital receipts funding 
arrangements previously in place.  It is proposed to utilise £9.4m of capital 
receipts to: 
• support the on-going asset disposal programme. 
• to fund expenditure related to Statutory redundancy and pension costs 
when they are a result of restructures that generate savings that pay 
back within three years. 
• Support the AI & Digital strategy, specifically transformation related 
projects. 
• Deliver the Council’s new Finance and HR system (ERP). 
78. Council is asked to approve the latest strategy for 2025/26 which has been 
updated to reflect latest plans. 
79. Appendix 13 sets out the strategy for consideration and approval by 
Council.  The strategy includes details of the proposed schemes for 

=== PDF PAGE 17 ===
2026/27, along with an update on the 2024/25 outturn and the latest plan for 
2025/26. 
80. To facilitate decision making it is recommended to delegate authority for 
amending the Flexible Use of Capital Receipts strategy to the Chief Finance 
Officer, in consultation with the Cabinet Member for Finance and 
Procurement, with any changes to be reported through the quarterly 
Revenue Monitoring to Cabinet during the year. 
 
Budget Consultation 
81. The Council’s 2026/27 Budget Consultation was open from 11 December 
2025 to 11 January 2026 with a questionnaire which was designed to: 
i. Seek views on how respondents prioritise the wide range of services 
provided by the Council and on the Council’s approach to addressing 
the budget gap. 
ii. Ask for information on age, gender, ethnicity, and religious beliefs of 
respondents to help segment the results.  The sharing of this 
information was entirely optional for respondents. 
82. The budget consultation was advertised on the Council’s website, posted on 
social media (Facebook, X, Instagram and Nextdoor), promoted through 
various e-newsletters and on electronic notice boards across the housing 
estates. 
83. In total, 101 responses were received.  Most responses were from 
individual residents, but responses were also received from the following 
organisations:- Carers and Parents of adults with learning disabilities in 
Enfield (CAPE) and Enfield Food Partnerships. 
Results of the Budget Consultation 
84. Highway Maintenance (97%), Refuse and recycling (95%) and Street 
cleansing (95%), were the top priorities where respondents thought it was 
important (either fairly or very) to continue prioritising spend.  Ranking just 
below these came Parks, open spaces & playgrounds (89%) and 
Community Safety (88%). 
85. Regarding the question on the Council’s approach to addressing the budget 
gap, the response was almost equal, with 35% agreeing and 36% 
disagreeing with the approach which may reflect the difficult decisions the 
Council has to make, since a wide range of services are considered as 
priorities for many residents. 
86. Detailed analysis and themes from the budget consultation is included in 
Appendix 14a of this report. 

=== PDF PAGE 18 ===

Dedicated Schools Grant (DSG) and the schools Budget 
94. The 2026/27 DSG settlement was published on 17 December 2025, 
confirming the initial DSG allocations.  The DSG continues to be funded via 
four blocks as previously:  
• Schools block – costs of primary and secondary mainstream 
education for pupils up to age 16 in maintained schools and 
academies 
• Central schools’ services block (CSSB) – costs of some services for 
schools delivered by the Council 
• High needs block – educational costs of provision for children and 
young people with special educational needs and disabilities (SEND), 
from ages 0 to 25, and alternative provision (AP) for children of 
compulsory school age, who, because of exclusion, illness, or other 
reasons, cannot receive their education in mainstream schools 
• Early years block – the educational costs of early years provision  
95. The main DSG funding changes made by the Government in 2026/27 were:  
• National high needs funding formula (NFF) has been suspended for 
2026/27, with the DSG high needs block funding based on the 
2025/26 allocation 
• Incorporation of the school budget support grant (SBSG), National 
Insurance contributions (NICs) and core schools budget grant (CSBG) 
funding into the DSG allocations 
96. The total DSG allocation before recoupment and other adjustments for 
Enfield is £483.4m for 2026/27 as summarised in table 9.  
Table 9: DSG 2025/26 to 2026/27

School block 
98. The Enfield Schools Block DSG allocation before recoupment and other 
adjustments is £338.7m, an overall decrease of £2.8m or 0.83%, after the 
impact of overall pupil number reductions.

from the previous October school census, summarised in the table below.

100. The total schools block allocation is calculated based on primary and 
secondary units of funding, premises funding and growth funding. In 
addition, grant funding has been rolled into the school national funding 
formula in 2026/27.  
101. The Schools Forum approved the transfer of 0.5% of the schools’ block 
allocation to the high needs block at the meeting on 3 December 2025. The 
remainder of funding is allocated to mainstream schools through the school 
funding formula. 
102. The draft school funding formula and budget has been calculated using the 
pupil and school characteristics from the 2026/27 Department for Education 
(DfE) Authority Proforma Tool (APT), based on the October 2025 census 
dataset and the National Funding Formula unit rates. No changes were 
made to the principles and methodology applied in 2025/26. This resulted in 
an affordability gap of £3.1m.  which has been addressed by adjusting the 
Basic Entitlement formula rates in line with the methodology previously 
discussed with the Schools Forum and Council’s DSG Board and prior 
years approach.  
103. The Schools Forum was consulted on the funding formula at the 14 January 
2026 meeting. Schools’ Forum members raised concerns at the meeting 
that the level of funding allocated nationally was insufficient and advised 
that they could not support the agreement of the budgets.   
104. The Council is responsible for making the final decisions on their formula, 
which has been agreed internally by the Council’s DSG Board based on the 
DSG schools block funding available, national funding formula rates and 
previously agreed principles and methodology.  
Central School Services Block (CSSB)  
105. The 2026/27 Enfield CSSB DSG allocation is £2.6m, an overall increase of 
£0.04m or 1.49%.  
106. The CSSB funding is allocated to the Council to carry out central functions 
on behalf of maintained schools and academies, based on ongoing 

=== PDF PAGE 23 ===
responsibilities and historic commitments (funding continues to be reduced 
annually by 20%). 
High needs block 
107. The 2026/27 Enfield high needs block DSG allocation, before estimated 
deductions for direct funding of high needs places by the DfE is £89.3m, an 
overall increase of £5.3m or 6.3%. This increase is due to the incorporation 
of previous grant funding allocations into the DSG funding allocation in 
2026/27, so in effect there is no increase to the amount of funding received 
by the Council for high needs.  
108. Enfield’s high needs budget continues to experience ongoing budget 
pressures due to increases in costs, demand and complexity of needs with 
a cumulative DSG deficit forecast of £23.0m reported as at quarter 3. There 
is a risk that the deficit will increase significantly in 2026/27 as there has 
been no increase in the baseline high needs funding allocation.  
109. The Government has confirmed that it will review the methodology for 
allocating high needs funding following the wider reforms to the SEND 
system, which are expected to be set out in early 2026, to support SEND 
reforms.  
Early years block 
110. The 2026/27 early years block DSG allocation is £52.7m, an increase of 
£8.3m or 18.57% compared to 2025/26. This is an estimated allocation, with 
funding moving to a termly funding system for all early years funding 
streams for the 2026/27 financial year.  
High Needs Stability Grant 
111. This new funding was announced in the final settlement, where the grant 
will cover 90% of eligible high needs deficit at the end of 2025/26.  The 
eligible deficit is yet to be determined and will only cover the high needs 
(not all blocks) based on an assessment of statutory returns i.e. S251 and 
Revenue Outturn and dependent on the submission of a Local SEND 
reform plan. 
112. Funding will be received in the autumn of 2026 and will need to be put in 
reserve until the DSG deficit statutory override ceases to have effect in 
March 2028.  The government recognises SEND reform will take time to 
fully embed and that local authorities will need further support.  For deficits 
that arise in 2026/27 and 2027/28, local authorities can expect that 
Government will continue to take an appropriate and proportionate 
approach, though it will not be unlimited. 
 
Council Subsidiary Companies 
113. The Council holds significant investments in two wholly owned trading 
companies: Energetik and Housing Gateway Ltd (HGL). Energetik, 
operating under the Lee Valley Heat Network, building heat network 
infrastructure, while HGL aims to reduce the council’s homelessness 

=== PDF PAGE 24 ===
pressures. These investments align with the Council's strategic goals of 
promoting environmental sustainability and addressing housing needs.  
114. The Council has made available significant loan facilities to both Energetik 
and HGL.  The loan agreements have been set up so that the subsidiary 
meets the interest cost to the Council of any loans advanced and make 
principal repayments at least equal to the cost of the Minimum Revenue 
Provision which would otherwise be chargeable to the Council’s revenue 
budgets.   Further details are available in the Treasury Management 
Strategy report that appears elsewhere on the agenda. 
115. It is important that the appropriate risk management and scrutiny of the 
companies’ progress against approved Business Plans and financial health 
is undertaken to ensure that there is no risk to the companies servicing their 
associated debt costs. 
 
Preferred Option and Reasons for Preferred Option 
116. To set the Council’s Budget Requirement and level of Council Tax for 
2026/27 within the timescales set out in legislation.  The Council has a 
statutory duty to approve a balanced budget for 2026/27 along with 
consideration of the finances over the medium term and the Council’s 
reserves and balances. It is essential that there is a clear understanding of 
the anticipated income and expenditure flows for the Council, despite the 
uncertainties, and for savings proposals to ensure full delivery from 1 April 
2026 wherever possible to maximise their contribution to addressing the 
financial pressures in 2026/27 and beyond. 
 
Alternative Options Considered 
117. The Council operates a budget planning and consultation process during 
which a wide range of options are considered in detail before 
recommendations are made. Issues raised and discussed have contributed 
to this report including information from the Budget Consultation exercise 
set out elsewhere in this report. As part of its planning for both 2026/27 and 
future years the Council has considered future levels of Council Tax.  
Further formal decisions may be required in respect of certain savings, 
incomes and fees and charges proposals and, where necessary, such 
decisions will be subject to consultation and equality impact assessments. 
Relevance to the Council Plans and Strategies 
118. The primary purpose of the development of the Budget and Medium-Term 
Financial Plan is to direct resources to deliver the priorities set out in the 
Council’s Plan 2023-26: 
• Clean and green places 
• Strong, healthy, and safe communities 
• Thriving children and young people 
• More and better homes 
• An economy that works for everyone 

=== PDF PAGE 25 ===
119. As part of this, one of the principles of the Council Plan 2023-26 is financial 
resilience. 
120. The Plan emphasises the need for financial resilience and moving to a 
position where we are less reliant on central government funding and 
instead resourced more by funding generated in-borough (e.g., our Council 
Tax base and National Non-Domestic Rates), and through inward 
investment using levers such as our Local Plan. 
Financial Implications 
121. The Local Government Act 2003 places a duty on the Chief Finance Officer 
to report to Council as part of the budget process on the robustness of the 
estimates and the adequacy of the proposed financial reserves. Statutory 
guidance in this area is provided by Local Authority Accounting Panel (LAAP) 
Bulletin 77 (Nov 2008) and is the basis on which the Chief Finance Officer’s 
annual financial risk assessment has been updated in the Council Budget 
report. A full statement of robustness is provided at Appendix 2.  
122. The 2026/27 budget has been prepared considering the following: 
(i)  Specific cost pressures set out in paragraphs 44 to 49. 
(ii) The final local government finance settlement. 
(iii) Provision for legislative change and changes to the Council’s 
statutory responsibilities; and  
(iv) The estimated impact of underlying cost pressures, as evidenced by 
financial monitoring reports in the current year.  
Legal Implications 
123. Under section 31A of the Local Government Finance Act 1992, budget 
calculations must be made before 11th March, although they are not invalid 
if made after that date. Any delay in setting the Council Tax could have 
serious financial consequences for the Council and tax payers, although no 
sum is payable for Council tax until 14 days after the posting of bills. 
124. The setting of the budget is a matter for the Council, having considered 
recommendations by the Cabinet. The Council’s budget-setting process is 
set out in the Constitution. Before the final recommendations are made to 
the Council, an Overview and Scrutiny Committee must have been given an 
opportunity to scrutinise the proposals and the Cabinet must have taken 
any comments of the OSC into account when making these proposals. 
125. The amount of Council Tax must be sufficient to meet the Council’s legal 
and financial commitments, ensure the proper discharge of its statutory 
duties and lead to a balanced budget. Members should be mindful of their 
fiduciary duty to ratepayers when adopting a budget and setting a Council 
Tax. 
126. The Council is required by section 33 of the Local Government Finance Act 
1992 (the Act) to calculate for each financial year the basic amount of its 
Council Tax. The basic amount of Council Tax must be calculated from the 
Council Tax base. This base is created by applying a prescribed formula 
under the Local Authorities (Calculation of Council Tax Base) Regulations 
2012. 

=== PDF PAGE 26 ===
127. The Council is required to submit its Council Tax base to the GLA between 
1 December and 31 January in the financial year preceding the financial 
year for which the calculation of Council Tax base is made. If the Council 
does not submit its Council Tax base to the Greater Local Authority (GLA), 
then the GLA is required to determine the calculation for itself, in the 
manner prescribed in the above Regulations. 
128. Members are obliged to consider all relevant considerations and disregard 
all irrelevant considerations in seeking to ensure that the Council acts 
lawfully in adopting a budget and setting Council Tax. Members should note 
that where a service is provided pursuant to a statutory duty, the Council 
cannot fail to discharge it properly. Where there is discretion as to how to 
discharge duties, that discretion should be exercised reasonably. 
129. The Council is obliged by section 151 of the Local Government Act 1972 to 
make proper arrangements for the management of its financial affairs. It is 
consistent with sound financial management and the Council’s obligation 
under section 151 for the Council to adopt and monitor a Medium-Term 
Financial Strategy as this informs the budget process and is viewed as a 
related function.  
130. Under section 25 of the Local Government Act 2003 the Chief Finance 
Officer is required to report to the authority on the robustness of the 
estimates made for the purposes of the calculations required to be made by 
the Council and the adequacy of the proposed financial reserves.  
131. The Council is required to carry out appropriate budget consultation. In 
particular there is a duty to consult with representatives of Non-Domestic 
Ratepayers about the proposed revenue and capital expenditure before the 
council tax requirement is calculated. Feedback from the consultation has 
been taken into account in the preparation of the proposed budget and is 
reflected in this report. Further consultation and assessment of any equality 
impacts will be undertaken where necessary and further formal decision 
making will then follow. In the event that any savings or income proposals 
indicated in this report are not pursued further then alternative proposals will 
need to be developed, consulted and approved in order to remain with the 
agreed budget framework. 
132. In considering the budget for 2026/27, the Council must have due regard to 
its ongoing duties under the Equality Act 2010. The Equality Act 2010 
requires the council in the exercise of its functions to have due regard to the 
need to avoid discrimination and other unlawful conduct under the Act, the 
need to promote equality of opportunity and the need to foster good 
relations between people who share a protected characteristic and those 
who do not (the public sector equality duty). The Council should consider 
how its decisions will contribute towards meeting these duties, against other 
relevant circumstances such as economic and practical considerations. The 
relevant departments should undertake detailed impact assessments of 
major proposals to ensure that any proposals for savings are reasonable 
and meet Equality Act duties. 
133. Notice of the amount set for Council tax must be published in at least one 
newspaper within 21 days of the Council’s decision. 
134. Members’ attention is also be drawn to the effect of the Local Authorities 
(Standing Orders) (England)(Amendment) Regulations 2014.  Where any 

=== PDF PAGE 27 ===
vote is taken at a budget decision meeting of the authority, the minutes of 
the meeting must record the names of all Councillors present at the vote 
and how each Councillor voted (for or against) or the fact that they 
abstained from voting).  
135. Finally, Members should have regard to s106 of the Local Government 
Finance Act 1992 which provides that members who are in arrears on their 
Council Tax for two or more months must disclose the fact of their arrears 
(though they are not required to declare the amount)  and may not vote on 
matters concerning the level of Council Tax or the administration of it. 
Members should also consider whether they have any pecuniary or non-
pecuniary interests in the report and should seek advice from the Monitoring 
Officer if necessary. Advice will be provided to all members prior to the 
Council tax meeting. 
Equalities Impact Implications  
136. Local authorities have a responsibility to meet the Public Sector Duty of the 
Equality Act 2010. The Act gives people the right not to be treated less 
favourably because of any of the protected characteristics. It is important to 
consider the needs of the diverse groups with protected characteristics and 
other specialist characteristics already agreed by the Council to be 
considered alongside the protected characteristics when designing and 
delivering services or budgets so people can get fairer opportunities and 
equal access to services. 
137. The Council aims to serve the whole borough fairly, tackle inequality and 
protect vulnerable people. The Council will promote equality of access and 
opportunity for those in our communities from the protected characteristic 
groups or those disadvantaged through socio-economic conditions. 
138. The Council undertakes Equality Impact Assessment (EqIAs) to help make 
sure we do not discriminate against service users, residents and staff, and 
that we promote equality where possible. 
139. An Equality Impact Assessment will be completed for individual budget/ 
savings proposals and any policy changes relevant thereto. These 
assessments will evaluate how proposals will impact on people of all 
protected characteristics and will identify alternative action or mitigating 
action where any adverse impact is identified. There will also be appropriate 
consultation and engagement with affected people and organisations as 
appropriate prior to any changes to policy or services or facilities. 
140. The 2026/27 Budget consultation identified the potential impacts on the 
wider community of the Council’s proposals to address the budget shortfall.  
To ensure communities from across the borough were able to participate, 
the Council produced an easy read version of a questionnaire for those with 
learning difficulties, details of the consultation activities were hosted online 
enabling the text to be translated, listened to and enlarged, and assistance 
was offered to those who felt they may otherwise have had issues 
participating. 
141. Participants were able to submit their views on the overall approach taken 
by the Council. Equalities monitoring questions were asked to enable the 
data to be cross-referenced with the opinions expressed by participants. 

=== PDF PAGE 28 ===
Workforce Implications 
142. Any proposal that is likely to impact on posts, structure changes and 
potential closure of services, will require the Council to conduct a 
meaningful and timely consultation with staff and the recognised trade 
unions.  This will include consideration of alternative proposals put forward 
as part of the consultation process.  The Council’s HR policies and 
procedures for restructures should be followed.  Any consideration for staff 
structural changes should ensure there is a resilient workforce to deliver on-
going service requirements.  Therefore, consideration of workforce planning 
should be included in the process. 
143. Where redundancies are necessary, the appropriate HR policies and 
procedures will be followed.  Redeployment options must be considered. 
144. It is important that services engage with HR at the earliest opportunity. 
Environmental and Climate Change Considerations  
145. There are no specific environment or climate change implications arising 
from this report at this time, these will be considered alongside savings and 
income proposals in future. 
Public Health Implications 
146. The Council’s core business is to maintain and enhance the wellbeing of the 
community. The financial climate has severely challenged its ability to do 
this. The MTFP outlines how the Council aims to meet its financial demands 
whilst minimising the effect of these pressures on the community.  
Property Implications 
147. There are no new specific property implications that arise from the 
proposals in this report.  As savings and income proposals are considered, 
property implications will be considered as appropriate. 
Safeguarding Implications 
148. None arising from this report.  Where there are service reductions across all 
services including Adult Social Care and Children’s Social Care, officers are 
working through these to ensure there is no impact on the Council’s 
safeguarding duties for vulnerable adults and children in the Borough.

STATUTORY CALCULATIONS AND RESOLUTIONS 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
The calculation of the Council’s Council Tax  Requirement is governed by the Local 
Government Finance Act 1992 (the Act) as amended by the Localism Act 2011. 
Subject to Members agreeing the budget in this report, the following formal resolutions will 
need to be considered by Council: 
 
1) It be noted that at its meeting on 21st January 2026, Council agreed the number of 
105,212 as its Council Tax base for 2026/27, in accordance with the Local Authorities 
(Calculation of Tax base) Regulations. 
 
2) The following amounts be now calculated by the Council for the year 20 26/27 in 
accordance with Section 31 to 36 of the Act as amended: 
 
(a) £1,279,795,000 being the aggregate of the amounts which the Council 
estimates for the items set out in Section 31A(2) of the Act ( gross revenue 
expenditure). 
 
(b) £1,094,921,000 being the aggregate of the amounts, which the Council 
estimates for items set out in section 31A(3) of the Act ( revenue income 
including government grants). 
 
(c) £184,874,000 being the amount by which the aggregate at (a) above exceeds 
the aggregate at (b) above (net revenue expenditure), calculated by the 
Council, in accordance with Section 31A (4) of the Act, as its Council Tax 
requirement for the year. 
 
(d) £1,757.16 being the amount at (c) above, all divided by the Council Tax base of 
105,212 (1 above) calculated by the Council, in accordance with Section 31B(1) 
of the Act, as the basic amount of Council Tax for the year 2026/27. 
 
(e) Column 3 in the following table, being the amounts given by multiplying the 
amount at (d) above by the number which, in proportion set out in Section 5(1) 
of the Act, is applicable to dwellings listed in a particular valuation band divided 
by the number which in that proportion is applicable to dwellings listed in 
valuation Band D, calculated by the Council in accordance with Section 36(1) of 
the Act, as the amounts to be  taken into account for the year in respect of 
categories of dwellings listed in different valuation bands.

Section 25 Statement       Appendix 2 
Olga Bennet, Chief Finance Officer 
 
1 Purpose 
1.1 The Chief Finance Officer is required to make a statement on the adequacy of 
reserves and the robustness of the estimates made for the purposes of the 
calculations. This is a statutory duty under Section 25 of the 2003 Local 
Government Act. 
1.2 This statement must be considered and approved by Full Council as part of the 
budget approval and Council Tax setting process. It covers all areas of the 
budget including the General Fund, Dedicated Schools Grant and Housing 
Revenue Account. 
2 Conclusion 
2.1 My view is that the proposals to strengthen reserves are adequate and that the 
estimates used in the budget are reasonable. Significant risks remain and will 
continue to be actively managed. 
3 Adequacy of Reserves 
3.1 The Council’s reserves have progressed from a critically low position to a low 
but improved position. This budget allocates a proportion of the annual growth 
specifically to strengthen risk reserves, aiming to reach adequate levels within 
the next three years, after which time the ongoing budgets will be released to 
fund services in-year. Safeguarding and prioritising these reserves is essential 
to maintain the stability of statutory services and those relied upon by the 
community, especially in the face of future financial uncertainties and inevitable 
pressures. 
3.2 The positive outcome for Enfield in the Provisional Local Government 
Settlement is an opportunity, in parallel with reinvesting in services that local 
people rely on, to move the organisation from a ‘vulnerable’ to a ‘stable’ 
financial position over the next year, and to ‘resilient’ over the next few years, 
and to take a longer term view. It will take ongoing financial discipline to ensure 
that this opportunity is grasped.  
3.3 Cabinet has previously agreed a high-level risk reserves strategy and defined 
the Council’s Minimum Threshold and Target risk reserves, which had been 
substantially eroded over the last five years. External auditors also underlined 
the importance of rebuilding reserves in their report to Full Council in November 
2025. 

=== PDF PAGE 33 ===
 
3.4 As the Council’s Core Spending Power increases over the next three years, the 
reserves scaled for the size of the local authority will reduce. Without action, 
Usable Reserves would fall into the bottom quartile of local authorities. This 
adds to the importance of fully implementing and enhancing the Reserves 
Strategy.  
3.5 Risk Reserves are forecast to hit minimum threshold balances by 31 March 
2026, and this budget prioritises proportionate action to replenish Risk 
Reserves over the medium term. I am satisfied that the proposals put forward 
at this time to strengthen the reserves position are adequate. 
 
4 Robustness of Estimates 
4.1 The 2026/27 budget has been developed through a comprehensive and 
multi-stage process, including the presentation of a draft budget in December. 
This has formed a sound basis for the estimates in this budget.  
4.2 The assumptions detailed in the main budget paper (e.g. pay award 3.2% and 
inflation) are consistent with sector norms and current intelligence but remain 
subject to volatility, particularly around social care and temporary 
accommodation markets. 
4.3 Key assumptions from prior years have been reviewed and, where appropriate, 
revised.  
4.4 The annual corporate contingency has been increased to £8m (1.7% of net 
revenue budget), closer to other boroughs corporate contingency levels. This is 
to recognise the risk that demand services cost pressures could be higher than 
current forecasts and that it is likely that some additional risk will materialise in-
year and it will not necessarily be possible to offset with in-year actions. This 
could be the result of the remaining risks detailed in the table below. 
4.5 My judgement in reviewing assumptions material to the council’s budget as a 
whole are listed below:

Forecast Meridian 
Water capital receipts 
Assumptions have been 
amended to significantly reduce 
the reliance on Meridian Water 
capital receipts over the next ten 
years. 
 
There is significant risk with all complex 
long-term infrastructure and 
regeneration projects. External advisors 
are assisting with developing new, 
detailed forecasts, but ultimately the 
value of each piece of developable land 
will not be known absolutely until it is 
market tested. 
 
Capital financing 
costs 
A new treasury system 
(CIPFALive) has been 
implemented with CIPFA’s 
support to ensure the 
robustness of capital financing 
forecasts. The system has been 
parallel-run with previous 
spreadsheet models to test the 
robustness of both. 
 
The capital financing costs and debt 
level includes an assumption of £50m 
General Fund Capital receipts being 
achieved in 2026/27 to finance the in-
year capital programme and 
transformation costs. There is a 
forecast residual balance of £12m of 
general fund capital receipts as at 31 
March (if none are used to finance the 
in-year capital programme or repay 
additional debt) which could mitigate 
any slippage in the capital receipts 
programme.  
 
Demand pressures All budgets, the most sizeable of 
which is Adult Social Care, have 
been right-sized, building in 
significant growth. 
 
The Council continues to experience 
significant demand and market 
pressures, especially in relation to adult 
social care and temporary 
accommodation. The forecasts included 
in this budget are the council’s best 
estimates at a point in time. 
 
Savings deliverability A ‘deliverability adjustment’ has 
been built into savings, 
recognising that despite plans 
and commitment to deliver 
them, it’s likely that a proportion 
of the savings are likely to 
achieve a part-year effect in 
2026/27.  
 
 
 
Some of the savings remain relatively 
high-level at this point with operating 
plans at varying stages of development, 
especially on departmental efficiencies. 
I am satisfied that the savings built into 
the budget are reasonable targets and 
their delivery will be monitored through 
quarterly financial monitoring.

Remaining risk to robustness of

Dedicated Schools 
Grant 
 
The capital financing impact of 
the existing DSG deficit (circa 
£0.9m) is implicitly taken into 
account by reducing internal 
borrowing balances.  
 
The Dedicated Schools Grant remains 
under significant pressure, with the 
High Needs block forecasting an 
in-year overspend in 2025/26 of £5.6m, 
contributing to a projected cumulative 
deficit of £23m by 31 March 2026. 
Given the cash-flat DSG High Needs 
funding, and nation-wide SEND 
pressures, it is highly likely that an 
overspend will continue into 2026/27. 
The council’s current estimate is that 
this pressure is £12.6m in 2026/27. 
This has not been budgeted for as the 
DSG is a ringfenced fund. It is not 
known at this point how much of the 
2026/27 overspend is likely to be 
funded. 
 
Energetik Auditors have highlighted the 
risk of Energetik loan payments 
to the Council. The Energetik 
Shareholder Reserve is forecast 
to increase from £2.6m to £3.4m 
in 2026/27 which could help 
mitigate loan repayment risks if 
they materialise in-year.  
The Council has on-lent £55.7m to 
Energetik to build heat network 
infrastructure in Enfield. The project is 
interconnected with the North London 
Waste Authority’s £1.5bn Energy 
Recovery Facility construction (for the 
heat source) and Meridian Water 
(reliance on connection fees). Delays in 
these projects would have cash flow 
impacts on Energetik, and in turn on 
their ability to make loan repayments.  
 
Energetik are developing the next 
annual operating plan to be presented 
to Cabinet to detail the next phase of 
delivering the Business Plan.  
 
Audit adjustments I have considered the 2024/25 
audit observations and 
recommendations from both the 
Value for Money and main audit 
report and am content that the 
council’s approach to 2026/27 
budgets in light of these 
comments remains appropriate.  
 
 
The council is recovering from a period 
of very little external audit oversight. 
The 2024/25 accounts audit provides 
some assurance of in-year 
transactions, but the accounts remain 
‘disclaimed’. It therefore remains a risk 
that significant accounting adjustments 
may be required as part of the 2025/26 
or future year audit which could impact 
the revenue and reserves position in 
2026/27. Because of the year-on-year 
impact (if they go back over a number 
of years), these adjustments could be 
very significant.

=== PDF PAGE 40 ===
Appendix 7 – Funding 2026/27 to 2030/31 
 
Funding Assumptions 
1. The outcome of the Government's Fair Funding Review 2.0 has seen a fundamental shift is resources to where it is most 
needed.  Enfield has seen the second highest increase in the government’s assessment of core spending power in the 
country (highest in London), with further significant increases in funding for the following 2 years also announced.  Please 
note the government will be confirming following years’ allocations on an annual basis. 
2. In addition, the government continues to simplify the local government funding regime through the consolidation of several 
individual grants into the Revenue Support Grant (RSG) and the creation of four other consolidated grants.  Further details on 
these grant blocks is provided from paragraph 21 onwards. 
3. The changes to funding assumptions in the MTFP are summarised in the table below with some further detail provided in the 
following paragraphs. 
 
Collection Fund 
4. The Business Rates and Council Tax assumptions in 2025/26 were prudent, given the uncertainty around Council Tax 
collection rates with the changes made to the Council Tax Support scheme, the cost-of-living crisis and high levels of inflation 
that were being experienced. 
5. Performance of the Collection Fund is routinely monitored throughout the year and latest forecasts as of December 2025 
indicate an overall surplus position of around £3.8m, Enfield’s share of which is forecast to be £1.1m.  This surplus will be a 
revenue benefit in 2026/27 and will be put into the Collection Fund equalisation reserve to mitigate against future fluctuations 
in collection fund performance and reductions in the government safety net for business rates funding. 
6. The Council Tax Base report approved by Council on 21 January 2026 provides full details on how the tax base has been 
estimated and set for 2026/27.  The following are key points from the tax base report. 
 
Council Tax including ASC Precept 
7. The provisional local government finance settlement confirmed that the core council tax referendum principles will continue 
the same as in 2025/26. The Core Band D threshold will be 2.99%, and the Adult Social Care Precept a further 2.00%. It is 
proposed to increase the council tax by the threshold level without requiring a referendum which is estimated to generate 

=== PDF PAGE 41 ===
£8.7m.  The balance of the increase in Council Tax in the table below relates to tax base and collection rate changes, bringing 
the total additional Council Tax income to £10.3m. 
8. The statutory calculations of the proposed Council Tax level for each property band and the formal Council resolutions 
required under the 1992 Local Government Finance Act are set out in Appendix 1. 
9. On 21 January 2026, the Council agreed a Council Tax Base of 105,212 Band D Equivalent properties for 2026/27 (104,291 
in 2025/26) based on a collection rate of 95.0%.  Total estimated income from council tax in 2025/26 is £184.9m. 
10. The government assessment within Core spending power also assumes a 4.99% increase, in addition to its own assumptions 
on taxbase growth.  The government assessment of taxbase growth is based on a 5-year rolling average of the council 
taxbase growth.  The changes to the council tax support scheme made in 2024/25 skew this rolling average, hence the 
Government’s assumptions are overstated and therefore the Council applies its own judgement on the taxbase. 
11. The Adult Social Care (ASC) Precept is designed to help local authorities with responsibility for Adult Social Care to meet the 
increasing care needs of their population.  The additional council tax raised from this precept must be used for Adult Social 
Care. Enfield has made use of this facility since 2016/17.  Enfield are applying the 2.00% ASC Precept permitted for 2026/27 
which equates to £4.1m of the £10.3m additional Council Tax funding. 
 
Council Tax Charge for Second Homes 
12. The Government has provided councils with the power to charge a premium of up to 100% on second homes where the 
property has no resident and is substantially furnished with effect from 1 April 2025 and an estimate for these has been 
included in the taxbase. 
 
Local Referendums on Council Tax Increases 
13. The Localism Act requires councils to hold a referendum for proposed Council Tax increases more than the threshold set 
annually by the Secretary of State for Communities and Local Government. The thresholds for 2026/27 should not exceed an 
increase of greater or equal to 3.00% for basic amount of Council Tax and 2.00% for the Adult Social Care Precept. 
14. For 2026/27 Enfield Council has chosen to increase the general Council Tax by 2.99% and raise a 2.00% ASC Precept to 
support the costs of ASC demand pressures.  This increase is not excessive as it is within the thresholds set by the Secretary 
of State. 
  

=== PDF PAGE 42 ===
Greater London Authority (GLA) Precept 
15. The GLA Assembly reviewed the mayor’s draft consolidated GLA budget on 29 January 2026 with the final draft budget due to 
be agreed by the London Assembly on 26 February 2026. This will be after the publication of the budget report to Council and 
so any changes to the GLA precept will be reported as revised statutory calculations and resolutions for approval by Council.  
The GLA budget was recommended with an increase in the Band D precept from £490.38 to £510.51. 
 
Band D proposed charge 
16. The Band D Council Tax payable by Enfield residents for 2026/27 based on the budget proposals (including GLA precept) is 
£2,267.67 as set out in Table 1 below:

Baseline Funding Level: Business Rates 
17. The Baseline Funding Level (BFL) is the minimum amount of funding a local authority is guaranteed to receive from the 
national business rates system, before considering how much business rates income it collects locally.  This will be made up 
of the Council’s business rates baseline, top up grant, and safety net payment.  The government’s assumptions for BFL are 
£50m for Business Rates and £91m top up for a total BFL of £141m. 
18. The Government have made some significant changes to Business Rates, and Business Rates Retention in 2026/27:  
• Revaluation 2026 
• New and revised relief schemes  
 
1 The percentage change shown is calculated in reference to the 2025/26 LBE Total Band D charge of £1,673.64 not the individual elements shown. 

=== PDF PAGE 43 ===
• New business rates multipliers  
• Move to a gross rates system 
• Revised s31 grant accounting for the collection fund  
• Business Rates Reset meaning that there are new targets within the system for each authority 
• Revisions to levy and safety net 
 
19. The Council’s forecast for business rates has been reviewed alongside advice from our collection fund advisors. The latest 
forecast as reported in the Council’s NNDR 1 return for 2026/27 is a total of £141.3m, made up of the retained element 
£49.2m, top up £91.1m and 100% safety net payments £1m ensuring the Council’s resources align with the overall 
government assumptions for BFL. 
 
Eight Authority Business Rates Pool 
20. The Council has benefited from membership of the 8 Authority London Business Rates pool for the last three financial years.  
However, it is not proposed to continue the pooling arrangements for 2026/27, since the business rates reforms and the 
upcoming 2026 revaluation mean there is no financial advantage in doing so. 
 
Government Grants 
21. From 2026/27, the Government is simplifying over 36 separate funding streams into a smaller number of larger, multi-year 
grants. The aim is to give councils more certainty, reduce administrative burdens, and allow more strategic long-term 
planning.

Revenue Support Grant  
22. Alongside the four consolidated grants, 18 additional funding streams are being absorbed into the Revenue Support Grant, 
totalling £75.3m. The increase in 2026/27 is predominantly due to the rolling in of existing funding totalling circa £52m. This 
includes areas like: 
• Social care support 
• Temporary accommodation funding 
• Housing and planning new burdens funding 
• Business rates compensation 
• Market sustainability funding 
• Share of Business Rates pooling gains (phased out over 2026/27 and 2027/28). 
 
23. This streamlining aims to simplify council finances and reduce the number of separate grants.  The Revenue Support Grant 
forms a core part of the Fair Funding Allocation (FFA) alongside the Baseline Funding Level. 
  

=== PDF PAGE 45 ===
Homelessness, Rough Sleeping and Domestic Abuse Grant 
24. This £9.8m grant supports the Council to prevent homelessness, reduce rough sleeping, and meet statutory duties to support 
victims of domestic abuse in safe accommodation.  This was previously treated as a service specific grant but has now been 
brought into core grants because this forms part of the Government’s assessment of core spending power.  It brings together 
funding for: 
• Homelessness prevention (including staffing and prevention/relief work) 
• Rough sleeping services and accommodation programmes 
• Support for victims of domestic abuse and their children in safe accommodation 
 
 
Children, Families and Youth Grant 
25. This £10.8m grant strengthens support for children, young people, and families, with a strong focus on prevention, reducing 
inequalities, and improving life chances.  It brings together funding for: 
• The Families First Partnership (previously Supporting Families) 
• Early support to prevent children entering care 
• Holiday Activities and Food (HAF) programmes supporting children during school holidays 
• Support for post-16 looked-after children through Pupil Premium Plus 
 
26. £9.0m of the grant relating to the Families First Partnership is now fully brought into core funding because this forms part of 
the Government’s assessment of core spending power and represents a £4m increase in grant funding compared to 2025/26 
funding allocations. A further £0.1m increase has been announced for the Holiday Activities and Food (HAF) programmes. 
 
Public Health Grant 
27. This £23m grant funds local public health services that improve and protect residents' health and is a £0.4m increase on 
2025/26 allocations.  The grant now includes: 
• Drug and alcohol treatment and recovery funding 
• Stop smoking services and vaping-swap schemes 
• Employment support for people with health-related barriers 

=== PDF PAGE 46 ===
• Existing public health responsibilities (e.g. health visiting, sexual health services, health improvement programmes) 
 
 
Crisis Resilience Fund 
28. This £7.4m grant helps people facing financial crisis and supports longer term community resilience.  This is a £5.5m increase 
on the funding allocation for 2025/26 and is ringfenced to provide targeted support to residents and communities in Enfield 
who are experiencing acute hardship or are at risk of crisis.  It brings together: 
• Household Support Fund 
• Discretionary Housing Payments 
 
Recovery Grant 
29. The Local Government Finance Settlement announced the continuation of the Recovery Grant for the 3-year settlement 
period 2026/27 to 20028/29. This £4.3m grant was introduced in 2025/26 and had previously been assumed one off but will 
now continue at the same level as in 2025/26. This grant is distributed to places with greater need and demand for services. 
 
Local Authority Better Care Fund 
30. This £14.5m grant is to support the integration of health and social care services, ensuring that people receive coordinated, 
person-centred care that helps them live independently and reduces unnecessary hospital admissions. This is listed as a 
separate grant for 2026/27 but from 2027/28 onwards will be consolidated into the Revenue Support Grant. 
 
Extended Producer Responsibilities 
31. New funding first announced in 2025/26 to assist the achievement of Net Zero targets through the implementation of the 
Extended Producer Responsibility scheme as part of the Collection and Packaging Reform Programme.  Enfield’s allocation 
for 2026/27 is £4.7m, an increase of £1.5m on the budgeted funding for 2025/26.  This grant will be classified as a service 
specific grant for 2026/27 because it does not form part of the Government’s assessment of core spending power. 
  

=== PDF PAGE 47 ===
Housing Benefit Administration Grant 
32. The grant for 2026/27 will be £0.965m, a reduction of £0.437m which is reflected in the MTFP funding assumptions.  Future 
years are estimated to reduce by £0.100m per year from 2027/28. This grant will be classified as a service specific grant for 
2026/27 because it does not form part of the Government’s assessment of core spending power. 
33. The Local Government Finance Settlement has confirmed that the Council will receive additional grant funding of circa £52m 
when compared to year-on-year allocations.  However, given that the MTFP had assumed that Children’s Social Care, 
Extended Producer Responsibility, and the Recovery Grant were one offs for 2025/26 only, the impact on the budget and 
closing the gap is an increase of £62m.  The changes are summarised in the table below: 
High Needs Stability Grant 
34. This new funding was announced in the final settlement, where the grant will cover 90% of eligible high needs deficit at the 
end of 2025/26.  The eligible deficit is yet to be determined and will only cover the high needs (not all blocks) based on an 
assessment of statutory returns i.e. S251 and Revenue Outturn and dependent on the submission of a Local SEND reform 
plan. 
35. Funding will be received in the autumn of 2026 and will need to be put in reserve until the DSG deficit statutory override 
ceases to have effect in March 2028.  The government recognises SEND reform will take time to fully embed and that local 
authorities will need further support.  For deficits that arise in 2026/27 and 2027/28, local authorities can expect that 
Government will continue to take an appropriate and proportionate approach, though it will not be unlimited.
