=== PDF PAGE 51 ===
Appendix 8 – Growth, Investment and Pressures in the MTFP 2026/27 to 2030/31 
 
Inflation 
Inflation has continued to vary over the last 12 months but is fluctuating above the government’s target of 2% (December 2025 
CPI being 3.4%).  This remains an area of risk for 2026/27, and the additional budget built into the MTFP for inflation for 
2026/27 is £10.4m, of which £2.2m is contract inflation. A further £0.6m has been included for energy cost inflation.  
Social Care inflation accounts for a further £7.5m (Adults £7.0m and Children’s £0.5m) and reflects specific factors such as 
both national and market developments and predicted wage inflation including National Living Wage. 
 
Pay Awards 
The Council has allowed for a 3.2% pay award increase in 2026/27 within the budget.  If the actual pay award is more than 
this budget provision, then in-year savings will be required to manage the pressure from within existing resources.  This is an 
area of risk for the Council’s budget and dependent on the final resolution of the pay award negotiations.  The 2025/26 pay 
award has been agreed and implemented for most staff (NJC & JNC grades). 
 
Investment 
The new investment of £1.0m included in the draft budget remains as was and this covers initiatives such as the Summer 
University, investment in Waste Services and Trading Standards, as well as improvements to cyber security and various IT 
systems for costs relating to the Council’s ERP system upgrade.  Since the publication of the local government finance 
settlement a further £24m has been added to the plan for investment.  This includes £16m relating to new expenditure related 
to ringfenced grant conditions for the First Families First Partnership grant, Homelessness, Rough Sleeping & Domestic 
Abuse grant, Public Health grant, Extended Producer Responsibilities and the Crisis Resilience Fund. 
 
In addition, to increase the amount of capital receipts available to reduce the Council’s level of debt, expenditure previously 
planned to be financed through the flexible use of capital receipts is being replaced with permanent base budget funding. This 
includes £2m for the Digital Portfolio and £1m for transformation (which includes existing invest to save schemes). 

=== PDF PAGE 52 ===
And finally, £4.4m is being set aside for early intervention and prevention of deprivation with more detailed plans to be 
developed over the coming months.  This investment is intended to transform the way the Council provides services and to 
reduce future demand pressures. 
 
Demography 
The demographic growth of £15.3m covers provision for ongoing demand in 2026/27 for Adults and Children’s Social Care 
and Special Education Needs Transport.  This is a £1.8m increase since the draft budget presented to Cabinet in December 
2025, reflecting the ongoing and increasing demand pressure being forecast in 2025/26.   
 
The most significant demographic pressure in Children’s Social Care is £5.5m required for external care purchasing due to 
both an increase in demand and the level of support required.  This area has been a significant pressure since the end of 
2023/24. 
 
£9.2m is required in Adult Social Care to reflect the increasing demand for services (in addition to the inflationary impacts 
described above).  This reflects the need to support more people, which in turn means that a greater number of financial 
assessments will be undertaken.  Part of the financial assessment includes working with people to maximise the benefits to 
which they are entitled.  Therefore, with increased cost pressures comes increased income expectations and these are 
reflected in the new savings and income proposals put forward. 
 
The estimated growth for SEN Transport has been calculated at £0.6m in 2026/27 and approximately £0.4m per annum for 
the following years of the MTFP rising with inflation over the period. 
 
Homelessness 
Through the Homelessness actions and mitigations put in place, the forecast overspend is £2.7m for 2025/26, reflecting a 
worsening position.  This has had an impact on the pressure for 2026/27 resulting in a total of £5.4m being added to the 
budget for next year.  The settlement has now provisionally confirmed the allocations for 2026/27 with the existing funding of 
£19.1m now being split.  The Temporary accommodation element of existing funding has been rolled into the Revenue 

=== PDF PAGE 53 ===
Support Grant, whilst the prevention funding remains in the new consolidated Homelessness, Rough Sleeping and Dometic 
Abuse grant. 
 
NLWA 
The estimated 2026/27 levy for the North London Waste Authority is estimated to be £14.1m.  This reflects an increase of 
£1.3m on the 2025/26 levy.  However, a £1.6m increase had been forecast for 2026/27 when the 2025/26 budget was set in 
February 2025, so there has been an improvement of £0.3m.  Forecasts over the medium term have been updated since the 
December 2025 draft budget report, reflecting the revised timescales for the North London Heat and Power project to be 
completed.  These types of situations were envisaged in the December draft budget where provision had been allowed for 
unknown and emerging pressures.  A corresponding reduction in the unknown pressures provision has been made resulting in 
a neutral budget gap impact over the five years. 
 
Concessionary Fares 
Forecasts for Concessionary Fares over the medium term are based on estimates received from London Councils.  Next 
year’s estimate is currently £12.8m, which means an additional £1.2m being required to add to the current cost of £11.6m. 
This is the status at the time of writing this report, but London Councils have stated that this figure is the maximum settlement 
and is thus likely to reduce once finalised. 
 
Capital Financing 
Budget growth proposed in the MTFP in financial years 2026/27 and 2027/28 was circa £4m in each year, driven by the costs 
of debt replacement and supporting capital expenditure for which borrowing is required and included an element for 
contingency. A review of the Council’s borrowing requirement, replacing the use of flexible use of capital receipts, plus the 
removal of any exceptional financial support application has resulted in further reductions in the revenue capital financing 
costs. The result being that £2.4m of this growth can be removed from the base budget for 2026/27.  
In addition, the savings put forward include targets for disposals of assets which reduce the capital financing budget by a 
further £0.6m in 2026/27. 
 

=== PDF PAGE 54 ===
Central Contingency 
A review of the central contingency has been undertaken, benchmarking the Council against other local authorities.  An 
increase of £5m to the central contingency to increase it to circa 2% of the net revenue budget is planned for 2026/27. 
 
Rebuilding Reserves 
As stated in the December draft budget report, if the settlement was more favourable than assumed then circa one third of 
any additional funding would be used to rebuild reserves.  The final budget updates the planned reserves and balances 
increases proposed.  These increases will ensure that the General Fund Balance remains at 5% of the net revenue budget 
and that risk reserves are rebuilt in line with the Council’s reserves strategy and target level of risk reserves. 
 
Other pressures 
Welfare payments and the welfare team have been funded via a contribution from the Household Support Fund (HSF) and 
earmarked reserves.  The £1.5m pressure in 2026/27 reflects the strategy of phasing out the use of reserves and grant 
funding and establishing a permanent base budget. 
 
Increases totalling £1.2m have been made to the Council’s Bad Debt Provisions (£1m Adult Social Care and £0.2m Sundry) 
reflecting collection rates of income. 
 
A Council wide increase in both the volume and value of insurance claims is creating a pressure of £1m.

=== PDF PAGE 63 ===
Appendix 11a - Enfield Council General Fund Reserves Policy 
 
Background 
1. Sections 32 and 43 of the Local Government Finance Act 1992 require councils 
to consider the level of reserves when setting a budget requirement. Section 25 
of the Local Government Act 2003 requires the Chief Financial Officer (Section 
151 Officer) to report formally on the adequacy of proposed reserves when 
setting a budget requirement. The accounting treatment for reserves is set out in 
the Code of Practice on Local Authority Accounting. 
2. Like all local authorities, Enfield Council continues to face financial challenges 
because of sustained underfunding from central government over many years, 
huge increases in demand for many of our services from residents impacted by 
the cost-of-living crisis, and rising costs. 
3. Reserves are one-off money. The Council aims to avoid using reserves to meet 
ongoing financial commitments other than as part of a sustainable budget plan 
and one of the Council’s financial principles is to stop the use of one-off funding 
to support the base budget. 
 
What are reserves?  
4. CIPFA states in the Financial Management Code that “the aim of the authority’s 
financial reserves are to provide funding for investment in future activities and to 
act as a safety net in case of short-term financial challenges.”  
5. Reserves are an important part of the Council’s financial strategy and are held to 
create long term budgetary stability. They enable the Council to manage change 
without undue impact on the Council Tax and are a key element of ensuring the 
Council’s financial standing and resilience. 
6. The risk of unforeseeable events and uncertainties remains high, and as part of a 
response the Council may need to consider using general reserves as a short 
term measure while making the necessary sustainable adjustments to spending 
over the medium term including replenishing the reserves used as short-term 
expedience. 
7. When establishing reserves, there needs to be compliance with the Code of 
Practice on Local Authority Accounting and in particular, the need to distinguish 
between reserves and provisions. The split of reserves into different categories is 
helpful because each category has its own nature, purpose and planned usage 
that can be used to determine the required level of reserves. 
 
Why do we have reserves?  
8. The Council plans its finances over the short, medium and long term so that it 
has adequate resources to deliver services for the residents of the borough. As a 
large, complex organisation, there will always be variations between our actual 
spending/income and our plans due to variations in demand, demographic 
change, changes in costs and the funding decisions of third parties as well as the 
need to deliver projects and investments spanning more than one financial year. 

=== PDF PAGE 64 ===
To ensure we can manage these financial risks, whilst being able to maintain 
services, requires that the Council holds funds in reserve to meet these costs as 
and when they arise and to deal with any emergencies that may occur. 
 
Types of Reserve  
9. When reviewing the medium term financial plans and preparing the annual 
budgets, the establishment and maintenance of reserves should be considered. 
These can be held for various purposes as set out below: 
• General Fund Balance - the General Fund Balance is essentially a risk 
reserve to cover unforeseen expenses, which should only be used in 
exceptional circumstances. 
• Risk Reserves - these are essential for managing financial pressures and 
uncertainties that may arise during the execution of projects or operations. 
They provide a contingency sum to cover potential pressures to ensure 
that the Council can continue to operate sustainably within an uncertain 
economic environment. 
• Meridian Water reserve - this reserve holds funds received as part of the 
development agreement for the Meridian One scheme. 
• Companies Reserves – holds resources to offset against possible credit 
losses associated with loans to Energetik in the future. 
• Insurance Reserve - the internal insurance fund provides cover in full for 
tree root damage claims, burglary and “all risks” on specified equipment.  
The fund also meets the cost of all claims within the external policy 
excesses for general building fire damage (including housing properties), 
motor, cash, and public and employer liability claims. In addition, there is a 
potential liability with a former insurer of the council which would be a call 
on this fund. 
• Service Specific Reserves – these are balances held for specific reasons 
e.g. the local election reserve accumulates a balance to finance the local 
elections which take place every 4 years. 
• Grants & Contributions – these will be balances received through external 
grant funding or other contributions to fund expenditure for specific 
purposes e.g. the Public Health reserve. 
• Schools’ balances - the balance represents the accumulated revenue 
balances held by schools. 
 
How are the level of reserves set?  
10. The level of the General Fund Balance is a matter for the Council to determine 
having had regard to the advice of the S151 Officer. The level of the reserve will 
be a matter of judgement which will take account of the specific risks identified 
through the budget setting process. It will also take account of the extent to which 
specific risks are supported through earmarked reserves.  The Council aims to 
hold a general fund balance equating to 5% of the Net Revenue Budget. 

=== PDF PAGE 65 ===
11. Enfield’s risk reserves are low and have been substantially eroded by overspends 
over the last four years. The current risk reserves are too low, and this leaves 
Enfield in a precarious financial position. The Council’s strategy is to return the 
risk reserves to a target level which will provide greater financial resilience. 
12. The target risk reserves should allow the authority to manage through realistic 
financial pressures. Target risk reserves set too high would present a lost 
opportunity to council taxpayers. Target risk reserves set too low threaten the 
sustainability of the local authority. 
13. The Authority recognises that, given the starting point, it will take time to reach 
the ‘ideal’ risk reserves position. 
14. In the interim, the Minimum Threshold risk reserves are set by taking into 
account: 
i. the highest two years of overspends over the last five years. 
ii. the next two years of ‘risk assessed reserves’ (as detailed in the February 
budget reports) and the General Fund balance, which is the most protected 
risk reserve. 
15. The Council has a cumulative Dedicated Schools Grant (DSG) deficit. This is 
considered in the target risk reserve level because it is a negative reserve 
balance. Current rules have extended the DSG override until March 2028. 
However, as things stand, at that point the DSG cumulative overspend would 
have to be covered from revenue reserves. 
16. Target risk reserves will be set as Minimum Threshold Risk Reserves plus DSG 
deficit and school deficit balances and exclude the General Fund Balance. This is 
because the General Fund Balance being used signals an authority in potential 
financial distress, with risk reserves becoming critically low. 
17. As part of the MTFP for 2026/27, the Council has created an ongoing budget for 
replenishing risk reserves.  This was made possible by the positive outcome for 
Enfield of the Government’s Fair Funding Reforms. 
18. If risk reserves were ever to be entirely exhausted and the Council was required 
to use any of the General Fund balance, it is likely that a S114 notice will be 
required as the Council’s spending is likely to exceed its resources. The Council 
would apply for additional Exceptional Financial Support before this point – but 
this would not be a sustainable solution. 
 
Management and Governance 
19. Each reserve must be supported by annual justification, stating the planned use 
over the life of the MTFP completed by the relevant budget holder (with Finance 
support where required).  These will be reviewed by the relevant Head of Finance 
before being approved by the Chief Finance Officer or delegated Director of 
Finance. 
20. Justifications should clearly identify contributions to and drawdowns from 
reserves, and these will be built into the Medium Term Financial Plan (MTFP) and 
presented within the budget report for approval by Council. 

=== PDF PAGE 66 ===
21. Reserves set for specific projects/schemes e.g. unused grant reserves should 
have an end date, and at that point any balance should be transferred into the 
general reserves. If there is a genuine reason for slippage, then the protocol will 
need to be updated. 
22. The creation of new reserves is subject to the same approval process set out 
above for the annual justifications. 
 
Monitoring 
23. The level of all reserves is kept under continuous review. This is achieved 
through revisions to the Medium Term Financial Plan and the quarterly budget 
monitoring reports provided to Cabinet. The planned level of reserves will be 
reported to Council annually via the Budget Report. 
24. The Council will maintain useable revenue reserves at 20% of core spending 
power as a minimum. 
 
Use of Reserves 
25. The maintenance and use of reserves play a key role in long-term financial 
sustainability. Just as the creation and maintenance of reserves arises in a 
structured way through the Council’s financial planning process, so the release of 
reserves needs to be subject to a similarly planned and controlled process. 
26. Contribution to, from, and between reserves must be approved by the Chief 
Financial Officer or delegated Director of Finance and from 2025/26 this process 
is managed through the Financial Control Board. 
27. When appropriate, details of any contribution to, from, and between reserves will 
be taken in a timely manner throughout the year and these will be reported to 
Cabinet through the quarterly revenue and capital finance updates.

=== PDF PAGE 68 ===
Earmarked Reserves Summary 
This summary explains the purpose of the Council’s main earmarked reserves.  
Comments regarding the adequacy of the reserves held are set out below while 
Appendix 12b summarises the forecast use and commitment of the reserves over 
the period of the Medium Term Financial Plan. 
Risk Reserves  
Risk Reserve Set aside as a contingency sum to provide financial funding 
over the period of the Medium Term Financial Plan for 
potential pressures. 
Temporary Accommodation 
Reserve 
This reserve is to mitigate the risk of an increase in demand 
for services and uncertainty in government funding 
particularly related to Temporary accommodation housing 
benefit subsidy loss impact. 
Collection Fund Pooling 
receipts 
This reserve holds the receipts the Council has received from 
being a member of the 8 local authority Business Rate Pool.  
These are then allocated to the general fund to smooth 
deficits on the Collection Fund in future years. 
Service Specific Reserves 
Companies Reserve This reserve holds resources to fund Energetik shareholder 
costs and offset against possible credit losses associated with 
loans to Energetik in the future. 
North London Waste 
Authority and Waste 
Projects Reserve 
Funding set aside to cover potential future costs of the NLWA 
contract arising from the building of the new waste treatment 
facility.  In addition, this has been expanded to cover all waste 
related projects and levies. 
Meridian Water This reserve holds funds received as part of the development 
agreement for the Meridian One scheme. 
Borough wide tennis and 
netball courts  
These are funds built up through annual contributions to 
enable sufficient balances to invest in replacement of courts 
in the future. 
Welfare Reforms & 
Hardship Fund 
The changes in the benefit regimes and cost of living crisis 
increases the risk of residents being unable to pay council tax 
bills and additional costs relating to the new benefit 
administration and regulations.  This reserve will be available 
to meet pressures in Emergency Support, Council Tax 
Hardship and Discretionary Housing payments. 

=== PDF PAGE 69 ===
Parking Development Fund 
(PPRA) 
This reserve was created when surpluses were made from 
the Parking Places Reserve Account (PPRA).  The balance 
represents sums set aside for expenditure on future parking 
and projects as permitted by legislation. 
Local Election Reserve Council elections are held every four years.  To smooth out 
the cost, a contribution is made into the reserve each year so 
that sufficient funding is available every fourth year to fund 
the local elections. 
Property Projects This reserve fund was generated from unspent dowry relating 
to the perpetual upkeep of Mossops Park, including the 
Mossop Creek and Delta respectively. 
Local Plan Reserve This balance has been created to supplement the revenue 
base budget cost of developing the Council’s Local Plan. 
NEXUS The NEXUS Project aims to tackle Youth Violence.  £500k 
was provided in the MTFP in both 2019/20 & 2020/21.  This 
balance represents unspent funding from those original 
allocations which is being used to continue this important 
work. 
Private Rented Sector 
Licencing 
Income through licences is generated which is then required 
in future years to cover the cost of running the scheme. 
Schools PFI Smoothing 
reserve 
Schools PFI funding is received in advance of the costs 
incurred. The reserve is required to holds balances to cover 
the costs in later years. 
Property Reserves 
Repairs Fund for private 
sector housing leased to 
Council 
This funding is set aside to cover the cost of repairs to PSL 
properties when the leases come to an end and the 
properties are handed back to their owners. It is “routine” 
business, with a low risk, and this reserve acts as a buffer to 
support the repairs work. 
Montagu Dry Lake 
Maintenance Fund 
This funding is set aside to cover future maintenance costs of 
a flood storage facility (dry lake). 
Other Grant Reserves 
Grant Reserve These are a collection of smaller earmarked reserves with 
very specific grant restrictions which limit their use. 
Section 106 Receipts S106 agreements are issued on development proposals and 
require developers to make contributions to support 
infrastructure.  These are like CIL arrangements which are an 
update on S106. 
Community Infrastructure 
Levy Reserve 
The Community Infrastructure Levy (CIL) is a charge which 
can be levied by local authorities on new development in their 
area.  It is an important tool for local authorities to use to help 

=== PDF PAGE 70 ===
them deliver the infrastructure and other community projects 
needed to support the new development.  In accounting 
terms, CIL is used to help fund the Capital Programme. 
Neighbourhood Community 
Infrastructure Levy Reserve 
This is a smaller specified element of the CIL set aside to 
support the demands being placed on their communities 
because of development. 
Public Health From April 2013 local authorities took on responsibility from 
the NHS for improving the health and well-being of their local 
population and reducing health inequalities. The Authority has 
been awarded ring-fenced grant since 2013/14 with specific 
grant conditions including the carry forward of underspends to 
future years. 
Troubled Families Grant The Council managed to achieve the maximum Payment by 
Results grant available and the reserve has helped to fund 
the extension of the programme as the funding from MHCLG 
in 2020/21 was not enough to cover the cost of the team.  
The remaining balance on the reserve is planned to be used 
when the grant funding ends to pay for resources within Early 
Help to continue for another year or until the pressure is 
potentially managed through the MTFP process. 
Rough Sleepers 
Accommodation 
Programme 2 Revenue 
Grant 
This is the revenue element of the RSAP 2 programme and 
will need to cover future revenue costs.  The balance is 
meant to cover 30 years’ worth of revenue expenditure, circa 
£50k per year forecast usage. 
TfL Risk Pot (Mini Holland 
legacy) 
TfL Mini Holland legacy funding to complete the 
implementation of the Cycle Enfield schemes. 
Southbury Synthetic Pitches 
Maintenance Fund 
Balance held for maintenance of the sports pitches at 
Southbury leisure Centre – grant conditions apply. 
3G Football Pitch 
Replacement Fund 
Balance held for future replacement of sports pitches.  This is 
a Football Association Grant and is subject to grant conditions 
in how it is used. 
Children's Social Care 
Prevention Grant 
This grant is for direct investment in additional prevention 
activity for children and families through the implementation 
of Family Help and Child Protection reforms. It will also fund 
local authorities to deliver against the planned new legislative 
duty to offer Family Group Decision Making (FGDM) 
meetings to all families at the pre-proceedings stage.  This 
will be held in reserve pending agreed expenditure plan. 
Insurance Reserves 
Insurance Fund The internal Insurance Fund provides cover in full for tree root 
damage claims, burglary and “all risks” on specified 
equipment.  The Fund also meets the cost of all claims within 
the external policy excesses for general building fire damage 
(including housing properties), motor, cash, and public and 
employer liability claims. In addition, there is a potential 

=== PDF PAGE 71 ===
 
liability with a former insurer of the council which would be a 
call on this fund. 
Property Insurance 
Settlements 
Insurance Settlement receipts relating to Sloemans Farm to 
be reinvested into Rural portfolio as per pay out conditions. 
General Fund Balances 
General Fund Balances This is a general contingency fund set aside for emergencies 
or to cover any unexpected costs that may arise and is set at 
5% of the Council’s Net Revenue Budget. 
Schools and DSG Reserves 
Schools Balances The balance represents the accumulated revenue balances 
held by schools. 
Dedicated Schools Grant 
Reserve 
The DSG is a ring-fenced specific grant, provided outside the 
local government finance settlement. It should be used in 
support of the schools’ budget for the purposes defined in the 
School and Early Years Finance (England) Regulations.

DEPUTYSHIP FEES
Remuneration of public authority deputies 
The following fixed rates of remuneration will apply where the 
court appoints a holder of an office in a public authority to act 
as deputy. These rates should be applied regardless of who 
carries out the function within the public authority
Category 1
Work up to and including the date upon which the court makes 
an order appointing a deputy for property and affairs.  An 
amount not exceeding £944
N
Category II
Annual management fee where the court appoints a local 
authority deputy for property and affairs, payable on the 
anniversary of the court order. Management costs are 
assumed to cover any incidental costs incurred in management 
of P’s affairs with the exception of those mentioned under 
paragraph 20 below
20. Public Authorities are allowed to use P’s funds to pay for 
specialist services that P would have normally be expected to 
pay if he had retained capacity such as conveyancing, 
obtaining expert valuations and obtaining investment advice
a) For the first year: An amount not exceeding £982 N
b) For the second and subsequent years: An amount not 
exceeding £824 N
c) Where the net assets of P are below £20,300, the local 
authority deputy for property and affairs may take an annual 
management fee not exceeding 3.5% of P’s net assets on the 
anniversary of the court order appointing the local authority as 
deputy
N An Annual management fee not exceeding 3.5% of net assets, where assets do not exceed £20,300

=== PDF PAGE 178 ===
  Appendix 13 
 
London Borough of Enfield: Flexible Use of Capital Receipts Strategy 
 
Our approach to Efficiency and the Use of Capital Receipts 
With effect from 2016/17 the Government has provided a general capitalisation 
directive to all councils. This enables the utilisation of new capital receipts to finance 
projects that are designed to generate ongoing revenue savings in the delivery of 
public services and/or transform service delivery in a way that reduces costs or 
demand for services in future years for the Council and any of the public sector delivery 
partners. 
The Government has announced that t he capital receipts flexibility will be extended  
until 2030.  This Strategy reports on how capital receipts were used to fund investment 
in 2024/25 as well as how they are planned to be used to fund investment in 2025/26 
and 2026/27. 
Within the Capital Strategy , the Council has set out its approach for 2026 /27 and 
beyond.  The capital receipts disposals target for the next 5 years is £130m, with a 
target of £50m set for 2026/27, of which £42.6m is earmarked for debt repayment and 
£7.4m to be applied under this Flexible Use of Capital Receipts policy. 
Capital receipts are therefore available to fund transformational expenditure that will 
deliver revenue savings to the Council to assist in bridging its current budget gap or to 
fund any future Exceptional Financial Support applications which may be required. 
Impact on the Prudential Indicators 
The Prudential Indicators are set out in the Treasury Management Strategy Report, 
also on this agenda.  These demonstrate that Enfield’s capital investment plans are 
affordable, prudent , and sustainable, and that treasury management decisions are 
taken in accordance with good professional practice. The indicators take account of 
the proposals for the use of capital receipts set out in this strategy. 
Based on the current capital programme, if the capital receipts were to be applied to 
fund capital expenditure, this would have the estimated impact of reducing the annual 
interest and minimum revenue provision in future years by £ 70k on average for each 
£1m of applied capital receipts. This is based on an asset with a forty -year life 
discounted on a 3.50% rate on an annuity basis in accordance with the Council’s MRP 
Policy and a 4.5% borrowing interest rate i.e. the equivalent of a 7% return for 40 
years.

Reasons for disagreeing with Council’s approach 
There were a wide range of responses, although percentage scores were low for each. Key themes (based 
on frequency of reference) detailed below 
Q5a. Please tell us the main reason(s) why you disagree.  31 participants responded to this question
Perceived waste or mismanagement of Council spending 
Money is being spent on the wrong things (e.g. benches, road -narrowing schemes and large regeneration projects) and budgets are 
poorly managed overall
Concerns about Council Tax and charges increasing 
Above-inflation Council Tax rises, parking charge increases and the belief that residents are being asked to pay more while services 
decline
Belief that savings should be found elsewhere within the Council 
Reducing senior salaries, cutting contractors, reviewing management structures and improving efficiency rather than raising inco me 
or cutting services.
Disagreement with spending priorities, especially around social care and benefits 
Too much spent on adult social care and benefits, while others argue social care is already underfunded and facing hidden cut s
Lack of trust or confidence in the Council’s approach
Scepticism about assumptions in the budget, concerns about long -term impacts, a sense that residents’ views are not being listen ed 
to and doubts about whether proposed savings are realistic

=== PDF PAGE 191 ===
Further comments
Participants were asked to add any further comments they wanted the Council to consider. A broad range of 
views were submitted. Below is a written summary of the most referred to issues
Q6. Please tell us the main reason(s) why you disagree.  47 participants responded to this question
Frustration with services 
Council services are difficult to access, communication is unclear and feedback is not meaningfully acted upon
Concerns about financial management 
Perceived wasteful spending, high management costs, and a lack of focus on core services
Local environment and infrastructure issues  
Dissatisfaction with street cleanliness, waste services, road layouts and the growth of certain types of business
Social care and housing pressures  
Concerns about cuts to social care and ongoing challenges accessing affordable, suitable housing

- 2 - 
 
The Leader of the Council, Cllr Ergin Erbil, introduced the report, providing an 
overview of the Council’s Medium-Term Financial Plan (MTFP) 2026/27 to 
2030/31 together with the specific draft budget proposals for 2026/27 which 
were considered by Cabinet on 10 December 2025 for approval to commence 
public consultation. 
  
Members were advised that this was the first year in which the Council had 
received a three-year Local Government Finance Settlement, which was 
issued by Government in December 2025. Due to statutory timescales and 
the budget-setting process, the draft budget had been prepared using the best 
information available prior to receipt of the settlement. Officers emphasised 
that the budget remained a live document, reflecting information available up 
to 10 December 2025, and would continue to be refined ahead of 
consideration by Cabinet and Council in February. Public consultation was 
ongoing. 
  
The net revenue budget for 2025/26 was set at £354m, with an indicative draft 
budget of £374m for 2026/27, as set out in Appendix A(ii) of the report. The 
budget was underpinned by a five-year financial plan. 
Officers explained that two funding scenarios had been modelled during 
budget development: a “low” scenario and a “favourable” scenario. The 
proposals presented reflected the favourable scenario, following confirmation 
of a better-than-anticipated settlement after a prolonged period of austerity. It 
was noted that the government’s estimate was that settlement was expected 
to provide approximately £200m of additional funding over five years, although 
some of this funding would be ring-fenced and the first year has already been 
received. 
  
Members were advised that significant budget pressures continued to affect 
the Council, particularly in relation to: 
   Temporary Accommodation, 
   Adult Social Care, with a projected pressure of £16m in 2026/27, 
   Children’s Social Care, with a projected pressure of £7m, 
   Pay awards estimated at £7m, and 
   Capital financing costs and contributions to the North London Waste 
Authority. 
  
Officers confirmed that these pressures were already reflected within the 
budget assumptions and were not new or unforeseen. A contingency had 
been built into the budget to manage emerging pressures, particularly in areas 
historically prone to overspend. 
Officers also outlined a proposed “rule of thirds” approach to the use of any 
funding received above expectations, whereby: 

=== PDF PAGE 194 ===
 
OVERVIEW & SCRUTINY COMMITTEE - 19.1.2026 
 
- 3 - 
   one third would be invested in new or enhanced services, particularly 
those addressing deprivation, 
   one third would be allocated to strengthening reserves, in line with 
statutory and audit advice, and 
   one third would be used to finance transformational spend currently 
financed by capital receipts (which would reduce ongoing capital 
financing costs and debt). 
  
Questions and Comments were invited from Members 
  
In response to Members’ questions regarding service investment, Officers 
highlighted proposals to invest significantly in Children’s Services, particularly 
in prevention and early years support. This was described as an 
unprecedented level of investment aimed at rebuilding frontline services that 
had experienced sustained reductions over the past 14 years. Officers 
emphasised the importance of early intervention, drawing on learning from the 
Sure Start programme, to reduce future demand for high-cost statutory 
interventions. 
  
Members queried the Council’s approach to Temporary Accommodation and 
housing pressures. Officers explained that work was underway to increase the 
supply of Council-owned temporary accommodation in order to reduce 
exposure to market volatility. A new 10-year leasing model was being 
developed with private landlords to improve stability and value for money, 
alongside preventative measures to reduce demand and diversify the 
Council’s housing offer. 
  
In response to questions on funding transparency, Officers acknowledged the 
complexity of the settlement, which included rolled-in grants and changes to 
business rates. Some elements of funding had yet to be fully confirmed, 
although certain ring-fenced grants — including those for Children’s Social 
Care and the Crisis and Resilience Fund — had been announced. Members 
requested clearer breakdowns distinguishing core grant funding from other 
funding streams, together with indicative timescales for receipt of funding, 
which Officers undertook to provide as information became available. 
  
Members raised questions regarding Council Tax assumptions. Officers 
confirmed that the draft budget assumed annual increases of 4.99%, which 
reflected the ongoing demand cost pressures and were in line with 
government assumptions on Core Spending Power. It was noted that, despite 
these increases, Enfield’s Council Tax levels remained comparatively lower 
than those of neighbouring boroughs. 
  
In response to concerns about savings proposals, the Leader of the Council 
clarified that not all savings represented service reductions. Some reflected 
efficiencies or technical adjustments, such as reduced pension contributions 

=== PDF PAGE 195 ===
 
OVERVIEW & SCRUTINY COMMITTEE - 19.1.2026 
 
- 4 - 
due to strong fund performance, reduced Housing Benefit subsidy losses, and 
the effective packaging of grant income. 
  
Members questioned the Council’s reserves position and financial resilience. 
Officers referred to the fact that Cabinet set minimum threshold risk reserve 
level at £43m and a target risk reserve level of £83m for the current financial 
year. Under the favourable funding scenario, the Council anticipated 
increasing reserves from the current level (forecast to be £47m by the end of 
the 2025/26 financial year) to the recommended level over a four-year period, 
reaching the target by 2029/30. 
  
Officers also explained the use of Exceptional Financial Support (EFS) to 
manage historic overspends, with £20m applied in 2024/25 and £10m planned 
for 2025/26. Senior Officers provided assurance that future growth in 
government funding would not be used solely to replenish reserves after 
recent overspends, but would contribute to support service investment, 
financial resilience, and long-term sustainability. 
  
The Committee NOTED the update on the draft 2026/27 budget and Medium-
Term Financial Plan and AGREED that its comments be reported to Cabinet 
as part of the budget-setting process. 
  
5   
TREASURY MANAGEMENT STRATEGY  
 
Officers introduced the Treasury Management mid-year update for 2025/26, 
outlining the Council’s current debt position, affordability metrics, and 
compliance with the approved Treasury Management Strategy. 
Members were advised that the Council’s external debt stood at 
approximately £1.34bn. This is forecast to cost the General Fund around 
£12m in interest in 2025/26 and approximately £19m is set aside for Minimum 
Revenue Provision (MRP). Officers confirmed that the General Fund capital 
financing costs as a proportion of the Net Revenue Budget was forecast to be 
8% for 2025/26, which was within the Council’s target range of 10–12% and 
therefore considered prudent and sustainable. 
  
Officers drew attention to the findings of the Council’s external auditors, who 
had recommended continued and enhanced scrutiny of specific large-scale 
capital programmes, particularly Meridian Water, Housing Gateway, and 
Energetik. Members were advised that further detailed actions and updates in 
response to these recommendations would be reported to Cabinet in 
February.  
  
Officers confirmed that treasury indicators, targets and ratios would continue 
to be kept under active review, particularly in light of the prevailing economic 
climate and interest rate environment. 

=== PDF PAGE 196 ===
 
OVERVIEW & SCRUTINY COMMITTEE - 19.1.2026 
 
- 5 - 
  
Questions and Comments were invited from Members 
  
Members sought assurance regarding the scale of borrowing and long-term 
affordability, particularly in relation to major regeneration and energy projects. 
Officers reiterated that the current debt financing ratio remained within agreed 
limits and that debt costs were closely monitored through the Treasury 
Management framework. 
  
In response to questions about Meridian Water and other significant schemes, 
Officers confirmed that the impact of debt on these projects was being actively 
monitored, and that further analysis and updates would be brought forward to 
Cabinet in February, in line with auditors’ recommendations. 
  
Members welcomed the emphasis on workforce development and asked how 
training would support resilience within the Treasury function. Officers 
explained that the use of AAT and CIPFA apprenticeships was intended to 
build long-term capacity, reduce reliance on external expertise, and ensure 
succession planning within the service. 
  
Members raised concerns about the timing of government finance information, 
particularly the impact of late settlements on effective financial planning and 
scrutiny. The Chair proposed that correspondence be sent to central 
government, including the Treasury and MHCLG, highlighting the challenges 
posed by late settlement announcements and requesting earlier clarity to 
support future budget planning cycles. 
  
The Committee NOTED the Treasury Management mid-year update for 
2025/26. 
  
It was AGREED that: 
   Officers would continue to monitor and report on the impact of debt, 
particularly in relation to Meridian Water and other identified projects, 
with a further update to Cabinet in February.
